The precompile prediction (`pso_predict_rest`, at a submission's first pipeline miss) saves the replay state, replays the rest of the submission's binds and restores it; in a 280-draw submission it made more than 64 composites, reused the slot the saved `S.prog` named, and the draws after it up to the next bind ran with that slot's new pair. The fusion probe and redraw and the deferred-draw replay restore `S` from copies the same way.
- **Fix:** each restore takes the composite again from the per-stage bindings it restored (`prog_resolve`, logged as `combined program taken again after a state restore`); a draw already under way keeps its `NvProg`, whose release is deferred. Every game.
- **Found on:** [AI: THE SOMNIUM FILES](../../dm/games/010089B00D09C000/README.md) (Unity 2017.4).
- **Check:** two runs each from one save with one key script, prewarm off: the old host logged the `texture type mismatch` line both times, the fixed host no red line, with the slot taken again twice.

### Compute programs bound apart from graphics programs

- **Symptom:** `no program bound before the draw`, `program has no vertex shader` (a draw after a dispatch is dropped); `the dispatched program has no compute shader` (a dispatch after a graphics bind).
- **Cause:** `BindProgram(program, stageBits)` with the compute bit replaced the one bound program, so a draw after a dispatch found the compute program (or none), and a dispatch after a graphics bind found no compute shader.
- **Fix:** the replay keeps the compute program apart (`S.cprog` in `nvn_mtl.m`, and `g_pp_cprog` in the pass analysis).
- **Found on:** [Hyrule Warriors: Age of Calamity](../../dm/games/01002B00111A2000/README.md): the game interleaves dispatches and draws; its intro movie's NV12 → RGB draw was dropped and the movie played black under its "+ Skip" prompt.

### Instanced array draws

- Instanced array draws (`DrawArraysInstanced`) carry instance count and base instance in the `OP_DRAW` record.
- **Found on:** [Super Mario Bros. Wonder](../../dm/games/010015100B514000/README.md).

### 8-bit index buffers

- **Cause:** Metal has only 16/32-bit indices.
- **Fix:** 8-bit indices are widened to 16-bit at replay into the frame arena.
- **Found on:** [Super Mario Bros. Wonder](../../dm/games/010015100B514000/README.md): its full-screen passes and its final copy use them.

### 8-bit indices widened on the GPU after a write in the same submission

- **Symptom:** a red line: the 8-bit index buffer had GPU-side writes in the submission (indices read stale).
- **Cause:** Metal has no 8-bit index type, so replay widens 8-bit indices to 16-bit on the CPU. When a copy, update or fill encoded earlier in the submission covers them (`pend_hits`), the CPU reads the bytes from before that write.
- **Fix:** those draws are widened on the GPU (`wide_run`): a compute pass reads the indices from a 16-byte-aligned address, with the byte shift as a constant, and writes 16-bit ones into the frame arena. As in `ixc_run`, the first such draw widens every such draw after it up to the next buffer write outside a render pass, so only a draw met inside an open render pass with no widening yet would split it. Draws whose indices no write covers keep the CPU widening. Log line `8-bit index GPU expansion`.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom](../../dm/games/0100F2C0115B6000/README.md): about one draw per widening, up to a dozen widenings a frame on the title and load screens, no pass split.

### Triangle fans and indexed quads

- **Cause:** Metal has neither triangle fans nor quads.
- **Fix:** non-indexed fans draw through a shared `(0, i, i+1)` index buffer with `first` as the base vertex; indexed fans and quads are expanded on the CPU into a 32-bit triangle list in the frame arena at replay (`nvn_mtl.m`, as for 8-bit indices).
- **Found on:** [13 Sentinels: Aegis Rim](../../dm/games/01003FC01670C000/README.md).

### Indirect draws and dispatches read by the GPU

- **Symptom:** indirect draws whose record the GPU writes earlier in the frame cannot be read on the CPU at replay; GPU-driven effects drawn as indirect quads, which Metal has no primitive for.
- **Cause:** `nvnCommandBufferDrawArraysIndirect`'s record `{count, instances, first, base instance}` has the layout of `MTLDrawPrimitivesIndirectArguments` (and `DrawElementsIndirect`'s `{count, instances, first index, base vertex, base instance}` that of `MTLDrawIndexedPrimitivesIndirectArguments`), but quads, fans and indexed draws with 8-bit indices have no Metal form.
- **Fix:** the replay issues Metal indirect draws from the pool buffer. `ixc_run` in `nvn_mtl.m` converts records of quads, fans and indexed ones with 8-bit indices with a compute pass into indexed indirect arguments in the frame arena, and the draw indexes the shared quad / fan index buffers (indexed ones get a 32-bit list expanded on the GPU). A compute pass cannot run inside a render pass, so when the first such draw is met it converts every such draw up to the next buffer write outside a render pass (copy, update, fill, texture → buffer, dispatch; they bump `g_wr_epoch`). Each record is then read after its last writer, and only a draw met inside an already open render pass splits it. `DispatchComputeIndirect` → `dispatchThreadgroupsWithIndirectBuffer`; when the shader reads the driver segment, the GPU copies the counts into c0 + 0x200 first.
- **Found on:** [Hyrule Warriors: Age of Calamity](../../dm/games/01002B00111A2000/README.md) (its GPU-driven effects are indirect quads).
- **Check:** `DM_NVN_IXCLOG=<n>` logs the first n conversions (record → arguments) once the GPU has run them.

### An indirect dispatch's arguments written by the GPU read on the GPU (Android)

- **Symptom:** Ocarina of Time's Kokiri Forest on a Galaxy Z Fold 8 and a Galaxy S25 Edge: the replay thread blocked a third of its wall time (13 ms a present at the frame step's hold 2, a wall-clock profile of the thread), the GPU idle meanwhile and its governor holding it at 500–600 MHz; "guest reads of draws: waited for the current submission" about 1.4 times a frame.
- **Cause:** `vk_dispatch` read an indirect dispatch's group counts on the CPU (`ind_read`, into the shader's c0 bank: `bank_c0`), and a culling dispatch earlier in the same chunk had written them, so `ranges_view` submitted the chunk and waited for the whole ring to complete first: a GPU → CPU → GPU round trip for 12 bytes.
- **Fix:** such a dispatch runs as `vkCmdDispatchIndirect` on the writer's GPU copy of the arguments (`fwd_find`, the forwardable write-back of a dispatch, in the chunk being recorded or one in flight, which is then held), and its counts are copied into its own c0 bank on the GPU by `vkCmdCopyBuffer` before it (the bank is made unique, since `ucopy` shares equal banks). Not for a kernel with spilled shared memory (sized from the counts on the CPU) or the upscaler replacement; a dispatch whose arguments no pending write holds reads them on the CPU as before. `DM_VK_IND_FWD=0` keeps the old path. The S25 Edge with hold 2 forced (`DM_FPS_TARGET=30 DM_FPS_HOLD=off`): 27.7 → 29.4 fps, frame p50 35.3 → 33.4 ms, the GPU at 832 MHz for 99 % of the window (774 before), the drains 7,326 → 4,262 a run; the Fold 25.3 → 27.3 fps. The dispatches whose arguments the forward does not find (18,600 of 25,600 a run on the S25; 2,100 of them waited) are the next thing to look at.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) on the Galaxy Z Fold 8 and the Galaxy S25 Edge (DroidPlay Core).

### A draw's in-place storage buffer write read by a dispatch where it lies (Android)

- **Symptom:** with raw global memory on, "guest reads of draws: waited for the current submission" about twice a frame with the GPU-side indirect dispatch on; `DM_VK_DEP_LOG` names a compute shader reading a storage buffer that a pending write-back of the chunk holds; the replay thread blocked in `ranges_view` while the GPU idles, its governor holding the clock low.
- **Cause:** a draw wrote the buffer in place in a placed pool (`gm_inplace_w`), and its write-back is only a note (the pages are prepared once the chunk completes). It is not forwardable: a render pass has no barrier inside it, so a later draw could not read it on the GPU. But the reader was a dispatch, which begins behind a full barrier, and `fwd_hit` answered as for a draw: the read synced on the chunk.
- **Fix:** `fwd_hit` binds the pool where the draw wrote when the reader is a dispatch, a draw run as one, or a task / mesh draw in compute form, whose kernels are dispatches (`g_rd_cs`, set by `draw_view`, `stage_set` and `vk_dispatch`'s argument lookup). The chunk counts such write-backs (`nipw`, `g_inflight_ipw` in flight) so `buf_fwd` looks them up when the chunk holds no forwardable write-back of a dispatch. The stats line counts them ("draws' in-place writes read by a dispatch"); `DM_VK_IP_READ=0` keeps the old path. `DM_VK_DEP_LOG` now names such a write-back "in place in a placed pool" instead of "staging (texture copy)" (its `st` is 0).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md)'s Kokiri Forest on the Galaxy Z Fold 8 and the Galaxy S25 Edge (DroidPlay Core): a 23,360-byte table a draw writes and the compute shaders 09ddd7f3f7560787 / 24f2e2c92ca45a52 read, the two drains of the ring a frame left after #371 and #381. MEASURED_PLACEHOLDER

### Indirect-count multi-draws

- **Symptom:** an empty G-buffer pass (the title screen showed only the UI); later, the submitting thread waiting for the GPU before each such draw (36–54 fps in Kamura Village on an iPhone). MIO also reported missing level geometry with maxima of 4,594 and 8,192 draws.
- **Cause:** `nvnCommandBufferMultiDrawElementsIndirectCount` / `MultiDrawArraysIndirectCount` were empty. The scene is GPU-driven: compute culling writes the draw records (stride 20) and a u32 count, then up to `max` draws (1–49 per call) are made from them. The original recorder also truncated every call at 1,024 draws, before the GPU-produced count was available.
- **Fix:** calls with a maximum of up to 1,024 are recorded as individual indirect draws. Larger calls store the argument address, stride, count address and maximum in a single command. For ordinary array and indexed draws, a compute pass masks the argument records on the GPU in batches, writing zeros after the live count. Consecutive draws in the same render pass reuse their encoder state. Maxima beyond the mask table capacity use the synchronized count to bound the work to live draws. The recording size for a large call is independent of its maximum.
- **Found on:** [Monster Hunter Rise](../../dm/games/0100B04011742000/README.md) and [MIO: Memories in Orbit, issue #941](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/941). The visibility-buffer shading issue in MIO is still open.
- **Note:** the replay used to commit its work and wait for the GPU before the first such draw after each GPU write, then read the count on the CPU (`mdi_wait`; still used when a CPU count is needed, including quads, fans and 8-bit indices). In Kamura Village that wait held the submitting thread 14 of its 20 ms a frame on an iPhone (36–54 fps). On the Mac the same scene's submit took 20 ms of wall time for 6.1 ms of work, and now 5.1 ms, all of it work.

### CPU reads after a drain wait only for later writes

- **Symptom:** a hitch of a second or more as a level opens: a few frames of 330–540 ms whose submission takes 2–3 times its CPU time, and the wait ledger (`DM_NVN_WAITS=1`) shows thousands of indirect-count multi-draw waits for those frames only.
- **Cause:** a draw with a geometry stage cannot be masked on the GPU (`mdx_run`): the geometry path reads its count, its indirect record and its indices on the CPU, after a drain (`mdi_wait`: commit, wait for the GPU) when the GPU may have written them. Which writes required one was decided too coarsely. `mdi_live` drained after any `g_wr_epoch` bump since the last drain, and the draw before had bumped it by dispatching its own geometry kernel. `geo_cpu_hazard` looked at every GPU write of the submission, including those the drain had completed. So each geometry draw of such a multi-draw drained the GPU once or twice: about 1,900 drains a frame in Donkey Kong Bananza's level build (35,775 draws in one frame).
- **Fix:** a drain completes every GPU write encoded before it, so until the submission ends only writes encoded after it can make a CPU read stale. Those are kept in a short list of ranges from the same sources the submission-wide sets have: storage writes and global-memory writes (`sbw_note`), and copies, updates and fills (`pend_add`). The drain also marks all argument buffers dirty, because `encode_args` notes storage writes only when it encodes a binding, not when it reuses one. The list is trusted only in the drain's submission, until it overflows or a write of unknown size arrives, and while every `g_wr_epoch` bump since the drain came from a geometry kernel, compute or mesh (`g_wr_geo`), whose storage writes are noted as its arguments are encoded. Transform feedback's writes are not, so it falls back to the old rule. `mdi_live` and `geo_cpu_hazard` read without draining when nothing on the list overlaps the bytes read (`drw_valid`, `drw_hits` in `nvn_mtl.m`).
- **Check:** `t_geometry_hazards` covers the rule: earlier writes stop counting after a drain, later storage writes and copies count byte for byte, a geometry kernel keeps the list, another epoch bump, an unknown size or an overflow drops it, adjacent writes merge, and a new submission starts without one. That is 39 checks, passing with early submission on and off; the 1,920-check geometry suite also passes. On Donkey Kong Bananza's scripted route the level opening went from 540 + 433 + 337 ms to one frame of 195–280 ms (the CPU-bound build), and the indirect-count waits from 18.85 to 0.10 a frame in that window. Frames through the opening and the first gameplay show the terrain, rocks, vines and props as before.
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md#found-on-this-game).

### `nvnCommandBufferDrawTexture`

- **Symptom:** a black screen while the frame loop runs at full rate, with `[RED] NVN unimplemented function called: nvnCommandBufferDrawTexture`; once drawn, the picture alternates frame by frame between the right image and another texture's (a solid colour, a still image).
- **Cause:** the call draws a texture region (texels, through the handle's sampler) into the destination region of the bound colour targets (pixels) without a shader of the game's, and the host had no implementation. Its handle holds IDs of the texture and sampler pools the command buffer bound, as a bind's does: read as IDs of the first pool, a game that draws from a second pool got that pool's textures at the same IDs.
- **Fix:** recorded as `OP_DRAW_TEX` with the handle moved to the bound pools, as `bind_handles` does (`hle_nvn.c`), and replayed by `draw_tex` (`nvn_mtl.m`): its own render pass per bound colour target after the pass in progress, the pending clears landed first, with the scissor and channel mask; the replay's analyses (pass fusion, deferral, clear hoisting, the store lookahead) treat it as a texture copy. Region coordinates map to the targets' rows directly in either window origin, since the host keeps the Switch's memory layout. Blending is not applied (`[RED] DrawTexture with blending on`), and integer, depth and compressed targets are not drawn.
- **Found on:** [Streets of Rage 4](../../dm/games/0100EC9010258000/README.md): its scene target gets the movie frame this way (the intro, the title's flames), from movie textures registered at IDs 256 and 257 of a second texture pool.

### Rasterizer discard preserves vertex storage writes

- **Symptom:** the HUD and sky render but the battle's characters and terrain are absent; the replay logs `draw skipped: rasterizer discard` between model draws.
- **Cause:** the replay returned from every discarded draw without active transform feedback. Rasterizer discard only suppresses rasterization: vertex storage writes and atomics still execute. Metal additionally requires a void-returning vertex function when rasterization is disabled.
- **Fix:** the offline builder adds `main0_discard` to vertex shaders with writable reflected bindings or image stores (`vs_writes` in `dmsh_build.py`: an image declared `access::write` or `access::read_write`). It retains the original body and early returns and drops only the stage outputs. Replay uses that entry in a separate pipeline with no fragment stage and rasterization disabled, with the ordinary array/indexed/instanced draw machinery and GPU-write tracking. Switching discard marks the pipeline dirty; discarded draws cannot prove an attachment fully overwritten. Pure vertex shaders with neither storage or image writes nor active feedback can still be skipped (`draw skipped: rasterizer discard with no vertex storage or image writes`). Transform feedback retains its capture entry.
- **Check:** `t_rast_discard` passes 19 checks under Metal validation: array/indexed storage writes despite an empty scissor and both faces culled, two instances, unchanged clear colours, normal rendering between two discard phases, and an ordered storage-write consumer. `test_discard.py` compiles a generated entry with side effects before two different returns for Mac and iOS, and checks which vertex shaders get one.
- **Found on:** [Xenoblade Chronicles 3](../../dm/games/010074F013262000/README.md#files-and-shaders); its note tracks the gameplay verification.
- **Also found on:** [Metroid Prime Remastered](../../dm/games/010012101468C000/README.md): image stores only. Its volumetric fog fills a 100×57×64 RGBA16F froxel volume with `1cbc19f806a08166`, 5,700 points with rasterization off that each write the 3D image; a full-screen pass (`1b17e5a457135e3b`) then samples the volume at each pixel's depth and blends it over the scene (colour × alpha + inscatter). Only storage buffer writes counted, so the draw was skipped, the volume stayed zero, and every scene (the Frigate's hull, the gunship in the opening) came out black while the HUD drew over it. Neither the CPU nor any other GPU operation writes the volume (its guest memory stays zero, pool flags `0x22`); `DM_NVN_TEXSNAP` showed the scene intact until the fog pass.

### Primitive barriers order vertex storage writes

- **Symptom:** exploding meshes and displaced model parts after vertex-only work; native post-vertex captures can contain valid positions for some meshes while other palettes are incorrect.
- **Cause:** `ORDER_PRIMITIVES` was omitted from the pass-splitting condition after shader storage writes. A primitive barrier orders earlier vertex work before later vertex work, including its storage writes. Metal does not supply that ordering between draws in one render encoder. Xenoblade Chronicles 3 uses barrier `0x21` after vertex shaders update its bone matrices; fragment-only barrier handling left dependent vertex work unordered.
- **Fix:** a primitive barrier after shader writes ends the render pass, as fragment barriers already do. The next encoder's full consumer barrier supplies the required visibility.
- **Check:** `t_rast_discard` passes 19 GPU checks under Metal validation, including a 4,096-vertex storage producer followed by a primitive barrier and a vertex consumer in the same submission.
- **Found on:** [Xenoblade Chronicles 3](../../dm/games/010074F013262000/README.md#files-and-shaders); `boot24barrier` removes the exploding meshes.

### Ordering barriers before a draw that writes

- **Symptom:** a character's mesh stretched for one frame into a long wedge or sheet that reaches another character, and other characters missing in that frame; intermittent (about one frame in a hundred near a group of characters). A pass dump shows `barrier bits 0x1 (render pass open)` before vertex-only draws (`fs=-`, `discard=1`) that bind a storage buffer `rw` which the draws before them read `ro`.
- **Cause:** a barrier with an ordering bit (ORDER_PRIMITIVES, ORDER_FRAGMENTS, ORDER_FRAGMENTS_TILED) also makes the earlier work finish before a later draw writes, so a write cannot reach memory those draws still read. The host ended the pass at such a barrier only when the pass already had shader writes ([Primitive barriers order vertex storage writes](#primitive-barriers-order-vertex-storage-writes)), so a write after it ran in the same Metal pass as the readers, where Metal gives no order between draws. Xenoblade Chronicles 2 skins each character with a vertex-only draw (`shd_post.wishp` var122) that writes its blended bone matrices from slot 0 of one shared 384,000-byte buffer, which the character's draws then read, and puts ORDER_PRIMITIVES before the next character's: one dumped frame had 91 such draws, 43 of them in a pass with the previous character's draws, whose later vertices could read the next character's matrices.
- **Fix:** an ordering barrier in an open pass without shader writes leaves a mark (`g_pass_order`: primitives, fragments), and the next draw whose program writes an image or a storage buffer is ordered after the pass's earlier draws (`order_draw`). When those draws read memory only in their vertex stage (`order_note`: anything bound in a later stage but samplers and driver data counts), or the barrier orders only primitives (NVN leaves the earlier fragments unordered then too), a barrier inside the pass from the vertex stage to the writer's stages does it (`barrierAfterEncoderStages`), and the pass keeps its attachments; otherwise, for a write in a geometry or tessellation stage, or in a merged or deferred pass, the pass ends first (Metal has no barrier from the fragment stage back to the vertex stage inside a pass). A draw without writes changes nothing. Ending the pass instead split Xenoblade Chronicles 2's geometry pass about a hundred times a frame (510 passes instead of 400, GPU p50 8.8 → 10.3 ms at Goldmouth Return Port); the in-pass barrier keeps 400 passes and the GPU time (8.2 to 8.7 ms). `t_rast_discard` checks six cases under Metal validation.
- **Found on:** [Xenoblade Chronicles 2](../../dm/games/0100E95004038000/README.md#rendering): the stretched geometry of GitHub issue #643, at Goldmouth Return Port and in the Argentum Bazaar.

### Transform feedback captured by a vertex entry point

- **Symptom:** characters collapsed into one thin spike (vertex streams read from feedback buffers that were never written); a character covered by a solid box while it moves (its fur shells pulled toward lagged positions that were never written); a pass dump shows draws with a vertex shader and no fragment shader (`fs=-`) before the draws that read them, and `Y tf begin … written 0/0/0/0` with no `T … tf capture` line.
- **Cause:** a game that skins on the GPU with transform feedback: a vertex-only program runs under `SetRasterizerDiscard` between `BeginTransformFeedback` / `EndTransformFeedback` and writes into feedback buffers that later draws read as vertex streams. The host ignored all of it, so the streams kept whatever was there. Metal has no feedback stage.
- **Fix:**
  - the layout comes from the program's control section (`tf_layout` in `dmsh_build.py`): +0x6dc u16 strides, +0x6e8 per-buffer output counts, +0x6ec buffer count, and per buffer b the output locations (0x20 + n) at +0xdc + 0x80 b and matching **byte** component counts at +0x4dc + 0x80 b. Multiple outputs are interleaved in that order. Unknown forms get a red line and no capture entry;
  - `main0_tf` (`vs_tf`) retains the body and stores the output bits into Metal vertex buffers 8–11, with the GPU-prepared capture record at buffer 14. The reflection lists `entry_tf` and `tf_strides`. Geometry programs give their output reader the same entry; it gathers the packed point/line/triangle indices while writing linear feedback records;
  - `nvn_feedback.inc` keeps offsets and counts on the GPU. Begin clears them; End/Pause save them in the game's opaque 32-byte control buffer; Resume restores them. A compute helper bounds each capture by all active streams and complete primitives. `DrawTransformFeedback` uses the saved count as indirect array-draw arguments. Captures, counter copies and consuming draws follow encoder ordering, and captured ranges enter GPU-write tracking. Plain vertex capture supports array point/line/triangle lists with or without rasterizer discard, including instances; indexed/indirect vertex capture remains open. Geometry capture uses the existing general assembly and compute path. `DM_NVN_PASSDUMP` prints the records as `Y tf …` and array captures as `T … tf capture`.
- **Check:** `test_transform_feedback.py` compiles generated vertex/geometry entries for Mac and iOS; `t_transform_feedback` checks interleaving, saved offsets, resume, bounded capture, GPU-produced geometry counts, complete primitives and a following draw that consumes the saved count in the same submission.
- **Found on:** [Pokémon Legends: Z-A](../../dm/games/0100F43008C44000/README.md): the character preview. 28 such shaders (e.g. `681cc42e48c0bbc0` in the `special/imgui.trsha` pack; skin indices in attribute 12, weights in 13) write positions (location 0 xyz, 12 bytes a vertex) and packed normal and tangent bits (locations 1 and 2, x only, 4 bytes) into three feedback buffers; the character draws read them as vertex streams 0–2 (`RGB32F`, `RGB10A2SN`, `RGB10A2SN`). Every buffer in this game holds one output from x on (strides 12/4/4, 12/12/12, 12/4, 12). The game draws these while its frame target is bound. The pass dump showed a pass of 19 draws with `fs=-` before the character pass. Tears of the Kingdom's 14,766 and Ace Attorney's 2,475 vertex shaders have no feedback at all.
  - [Donkey Kong Country: Tropical Freeze](../../dm/games/0100C1F0051B6000/README.md): DK's fur. While DK moved, his fur shells (an instanced draw, `648304c43bbe3463` / `5b5ffba62bca9e0a`, three shells on the body's index buffer) covered him as one solid box. Each frame a vertex-only draw (`6979dd3cdbd62373` / `7dcdaed991c74bfe`, 3,400 vertices) captures the fur's lagged positions into one buffer, two outputs per vertex (xyz each, stride 24; output count 2, component bytes `03 03`), and the shells read them as vertex attribute 6. Two things kept the buffer at zero (`DM_NVN_KEYDUMP=5b5ffba62bca9e0a`: stream 6 held zeros and `2.3e24`): the old one-output rule rejected the layout (`[RED] … transform feedback buffer 0 has a layout of unknown form`), and the game draws the capture with rasterization on (no fragment shader, colour mask 0, no `SetRasterizerDiscard`), which the host did not capture. Four of its 11,644 vertex shaders have this form; over Z-A's and this game's 23,277 vertex shaders together, the old and new readings give identical layouts and capture code for all 28 shaders the old rule took.

- **Found on:** [Xenoblade Chronicles 3](../../dm/games/010074F013262000/README.md#files-and-shaders): effect vertex and geometry programs use interleaved outputs, including eleven outputs in a 172-byte stream, and draw from saved feedback counts.

### Geometry-shader draws encoded a run at a time

- **Symptom:** a scene drawn with a geometry shader per mesh runs at 5 to 15 fps on the Mac, with the submitting thread or the GPU taking most of the frame.
- **Cause:** the general geometry replay (`nvn_geometry.inc`) handled each draw on its own. It waited for the GPU before every indexed draw (the indices are assembled on the CPU), ended the open render pass, and ran four encoders: the vertex capture, the geometry kernel, the index compaction in one GPU thread, and the raster draw reopening the pass with its loads.
- **Fix:**
  - CPU index assembly waits when pending or current/prior GPU writes, unknown ranges or sparse aliases prevent a safe read (`geo_index_wait`); native capture retains GPU ordering, and unique capture indices use a bounded bitset instead of `qsort`;
  - the compaction (`dm/gfx/msl/geo_pack.metal`) uses three blocked parallel prefix-scan stages (`geo_pack_count`, `geo_pack_scan`, `geo_pack_write`) with threadgroup barriers;
  - the first geometry draw of a run looks ahead (`geo_run_walk`, with state replay only) across the geometry draws that follow it with only state, query reports and event signals in between. It encodes all their vertex captures in one render pass and all their kernels and compactions in one compute pass. Each later draw of the run then only rasterizes its prepared output in the open render pass, omitting inactive colour attachments across the run when `geo_color_omit` holds.
- **Found on:** [NieR:Automata The End of YoRHa Edition](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md) and [US 1.0.3](../../dm/games/010056B015FE8000/README.md): about 70 index-build draws a frame in the flight section and up to 932 in heavy outdoor combat.

### Helper encoders end the open copy encoder first

- **Symptom:** `SIGSEGV` in `-[AGXG18GFamilyComputeContext_mtlnext performEndEncoding]` (from `rp_begin_ex_`, or in its `dealloc` on the encoding thread); Metal validation (`MTL_DEBUG_LAYER=1 MTL_DEBUG_LAYER_ERROR_MODE=nslog`) names it: "Command buffer already has an open command encoder".
- **Cause:** `tf_draw` opened its render encoder while the copy encoder (`g_blit`) was open, which the inline updates `ubd_land` copies there leave behind, as does a copy or a dispatch just before it.
- **Fix:** every other helper pass ends `g_blit` before it opens its own encoder, and now `tf_draw` does too (GitHub issue #101).
- **Found on:** [Pokémon Legends: Z-A](../../dm/games/0100F43008C44000/README.md): two minutes into a new game, about frame 3390 on the Mac, with the encoding thread on and off.

### Viewport swizzles applied as a flipped viewport

- **Symptom:** the whole picture upside down (Absolum's title logo and "Press Any Button" mirrored top to bottom); a red-reporting stub for `nvnCommandBufferSetViewportSwizzles`.
- **Cause:** the game keeps NVN's lower-left window origin (handled as for Link's Awakening, [Lower-left window origin](#lower-left-window-origin)) and, twice a frame, sets viewport 0's swizzle with `nvnCommandBufferSetViewportSwizzles` to (x, −y, z, w), which negates clip-space y: how a renderer with OpenGL heritage flips its picture.
- **Fix:** the swizzle is recorded (`OP_VP_SWIZZLE`) and applied at the draw as a viewport flipped about its centre, the same mapping. Facing is decided after the swizzle on the GPU as in Metal, so the winding stays. Other swizzles (axis exchanges, negated x / z / w) give a red line and are drawn unswizzled.
- **Found on:** [Absolum](../../dm/games/0100A83022A22000/README.md) (FNA3D's NVN driver, `FNA3D_Driver_NVN.cpp`).

### Black shapes on sprites when Unreal's GPU work runs late

- **Symptom:** on an Unreal Engine game with a full GPU, moving sprites flicker black for a frame: cut straight across, or a whole silhouette in the sprite's outline. It follows GPU time against the frame budget, and goes away at 30 fps or with GPU time to spare. The black is already in the scene colour the base pass writes.
- **Cause:** a race in the engine's NVN code. A dynamic buffer keeps `nvn.NumBufferedFrames` copies (3), and each write lock moves to the next one with no fence. The render thread can come round to a copy while draws queued for the GPU still read it. If that lands between a sprite's depth prepass and its base pass, the two see different vertices: the base pass tests depth `EQUAL`, draws nothing where they differ, and the cleared black stays.
- **Fix:** a workaround, since the missing fence is in the game's code: the rewriter gives each dynamic buffer a fourth copy (`dm/tools/ue_ring.py`, called by `patch_nso.py`), with no build ID, on any main where its chain holds. A buffer has room for exactly 4. The variable itself cannot be set to 4: its other readers size fixed tables, and the deferred-delete table has room for 3, so only the constructor's load changes. Open: this adds one lock of margin and no fence, so a GPU further behind, or a buffer locked more than once a frame, can still reach a queued copy. A single comparison has been run (5 black frames against 0 at equal load), and hit counts vary between runs. The fallback is copying small vertex streams at encode, which gave 0 against 10 and 14 in a diagnostic build; it costs every game a copy per draw and does not cover index buffers.
- **Found on:** [TRIANGLE STRATEGY](../../dm/games/0100CC80140F8000/README.md), [issue #1040](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1040).

## Uniforms, copies and ordering

### Bindless handles read from guest memory directly

- **Symptom:** a `sample` of the submit thread shows `ubo_read` calling `-[MTLBuffer contents]` with `objc_retainAutorelease` at every handle read, and `objc_autoreleasePoolPop` at commit draining those buffers. Fire Emblem Fortune's Weave: about 4 % of the submit thread in the reads and as much again in the drain.
- **Cause:** `contents` is an inner-pointer method, so under ARC each call retains and autoreleases the buffer; a bindless shader reads dozens of handles a draw. The argument writes in `encode_args_` asked the arena's buffer for its contents the same way.
- **Fix:** `ubo_read` in `nvn_mtl.m`, for every game. A pool's buffer wraps guest memory without a copy at its own address (`mtl_pool_add` is the only place pools are made), so once `mtl_addr` finds the pool, the bytes are read at the guest address itself; `mtl_addr` still runs, so the pool check and its residency are unchanged. An arena keeps its buffer's `contents` (`NvArena.cpu`), and the argument, push-constant, driver-segment and border writes go through it. If pools ever stop being no-copy wrappers, this read would need the buffer again.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=<id> run`. Fortune's Weave's battle map (MacBook Pro M1 Pro): submit 42.8k → 39.2k instructions per draw.
- **Found on:** [Fire Emblem Fortune's Weave](../../dm/games/04007EF01F8AE000/README.md).

### Depth plane copies between depth formats

- **Symptom:** `[RED] texture→texture copy case not implemented: 1920x1080x1→1920x1080x1 flags 0 format 260→252 3D 0→0`.
- **Cause:** the game copies its depth-stencil target (Metal `Depth32Float_Stencil8`, 260) into a depth-only texture (`Depth32Float`, 252) of the same size. A Metal blit copies only between equal formats, and the draw path does not take depth formats.
- **Fix:** such a copy, either way between the two formats, goes through a staging buffer kept from copy to copy: the 32-bit float depth is copied out of the source with `MTLBlitOptionDepthFromDepthStencil` and into the destination's depth plane the same way (`nvn_mtl.m`, the texture copy).
- **Found on:** [EA SPORTS FC 27](../../dm/games/0100C49025D3E000/README.md) (Frostbite): from the title on.

### Depth plane copies on the encode thread

- **Symptom:** `FATAL deferred encoding: DmRecEnc has no stand-in for -copyFromTexture:sourceSlice:sourceLevel:sourceOrigin:sourceSize:toBuffer:destinationOffset:destinationBytesPerRow:destinationBytesPerImage:options:` with Metal encoding on its own thread (`DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD=1`, the default on iOS), at the first [depth plane copy](#depth-plane-copies-between-depth-formats).
- **Cause:** that copy is the only one that passes blit options (`MTLBlitOptionDepthFromDepthStencil`, `enc_b_copy_texture_buffer_opt` and `enc_b_copy_buffer_texture_opt` in `nvn_mtl.m`), and the encode thread's recorder (`DmRecEnc` in `nvn_rec.h`) had stand-ins only for the copies without options. A call it has no stand-in for stops the host.
- **Fix:** `nvn_rec.h` records both copies with their options (`RO_C_COPY_TB_OPT`, `RO_C_COPY_BT_OPT`) and replays them on the Metal 4 compute encoder. Every selector the host's encoder wrappers send now has a stand-in. `check_t_rt_range` also runs with `DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD=1`; `t_rt_range` and `t_unwritten_store` stopped there before.
- **Found on:** the host's tests. [PR #930](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/930) added the texture-to-buffer stand-in for [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) on the Mac with the encode thread on (not run here).

### Indirect task / mesh draws on the encode thread

- **Symptom:** `FATAL deferred encoding: DmRecEnc has no stand-in for -drawMeshThreadgroupsWithIndirectBuffer:threadsPerObjectThreadgroup:threadsPerMeshThreadgroup:`, then `CRASH sig=6` under `mtk_encode`, with Metal encoding on its own thread (`DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD=1`, the default on iOS), at the first [indirect task / mesh draw](shaders.md#task-and-mesh-shaders-in-bnsh-and-indirect-mesh-draws).
- **Cause:** `nvn_geometry.inc` encodes an indirect task / mesh draw with `drawMeshThreadgroupsWithIndirectBuffer:…`, and the encode thread's recorder (`DmRecEnc` in `nvn_rec.h`) had a stand-in only for the direct one (`drawMeshThreadgroups:…`). A call it has no stand-in for stops the host.
- **Fix:** `nvn_rec.h` records the indirect draw (`RO_R_MESH_IND`: the argument buffer's GPU address and the threads per object and mesh threadgroup, as `RO_R_MESH` keeps them) and replays it. `t_rec_draws` checks that the recorder has a stand-in for every draw the NVN layer encodes.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md): the iOS app aborted when a save loaded; on the Mac with the encode thread on, 28 s after the title ([issue #1265](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1265)).

### Mesh draws with no task shader above 1024 workgroups

- **Symptom:** a GPU page fault (`kIOGPUCommandBufferCallbackErrorPageFault`) 30 to 40 s after a save loads, then the game hangs or [stops](#a-gpu-whose-access-macos-revoked-stops-the-game). `DM_NVN_SKIP_MESH=1` makes it go away.
- **Cause:** Metal allows at most 1024 threadgroups in a mesh grid. The object grid has no such limit. A mesh draw with no task shader has no object function, so its workgroup count becomes the mesh grid. Indirect mesh draws reached 1,771 workgroups, direct ones at most 32. A mesh grid above 1024 reads out of bounds. Draws with a task shader are safe: `dmsh_meshstage.py` clamps the grid they emit to `METAL_MAX_GRID`.
- **Fix:** a mesh shader with no task shader gets an object function of its own (`dmsh_meshstage.py` `main0_o`, the library's `entry_object`). Each of its one-thread threadgroups launches a block of up to `DMSH_MESH_GRID` (1024) mesh threadgroups and passes the block's first workgroup in its payload, and the mesh shader takes its workgroup as that plus its threadgroup ID. So the draw stays one Metal draw of ceil(count / 1024) object threadgroups, with no limit of its own: `mtk_encode` computes that for a direct draw, and for an indirect one `mdx_mask` writes it beside the task shader's `{count, 1, 1}`. The task and object functions read the draw's `{count, first}` at buffer 29 (`DMSH_MESH_DRAW_SLOT`): an indirect draw's record itself, a direct draw's copy. `t_mesh_first_use` draws 3,072 workgroups in three blocks, directly and indirectly, each to its own pixel. The object stage costs nothing measurable: Ocarina of Time's `kokiri-deck` route on an M4 Pro held 60 fps at GPU p50 12.0-13.2 ms against 11.8-13.4 ms without it, 3 runs each. An earlier version of the fix, which split the draw into 8 Metal draws of at most 1024 (pull request #1708), cost nothing measurable either, but stopped at 8,192 workgroups.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) on an M2 Max ([issue #1326](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1326), pull request #1708): the foliage's mesh draws. With a pipeline archive state that faulted every time at about frame 1,930, the game ran 14 minutes (frame 30,600) without a fault with the split. On an M4 Pro the Kokiri Forest deck route has about 19 such draws a frame, up to 1,056 workgroups, 28 above 1024 in a run, without a fault on main. PRAGMATA's mesh-only grids reach 6,623 (a test of the split in the pull request).

### Inline uniform updates as ordered GPU copies

- **Cause:** reading the uniform buffer from the CPU at encode time gives the frame's *last* value, not the draw's.
- **Fix:** inline uniform updates (`CommandBufferUpdateUniformBuffer`) are replayed as in-order GPU copies (their order inside one encoder: [Metal 4 does not order operations inside one encoder](#metal-4-does-not-order-operations-inside-one-encoder); inside a pass: [Inline uniform updates inside a render pass](#inline-uniform-updates-inside-a-render-pass)). They are snapshotted at encode time (`mtl_sub_update`): the data is copied into a host-owned ring instead of being read from command memory when the GPU runs.
- **Found on:** [Super Mario Bros. Wonder](../../dm/games/010015100B514000/README.md).

### Pending clears land before a compute dispatch

- **Symptom:** a scene opens near white and fades to its normal colours over several seconds, at each scene change, while the settled picture is right.
- **Cause:** a full clear with no draw after it waits on the current targets for the next pass that attaches them, as its load action. Copies land such clears first, but a dispatch only ended the open pass. So a clear before a dispatch landed after it. The game resets its auto exposure by clearing the exposure image to 1 and dispatching the shaders that read and write it as an image: the late clear overwrote the exposure they had just written, and adaptation then climbed back from 1.
- **Fix:** `dispatch` calls `rp_flush` as `copy_t2t` does. A dispatch moved ahead of a pass (`cs_hoist_try`) already waits for no pending clear. The cost is one clear-only pass where a game clears targets and dispatches before drawing to them.
- **Found on:** [Fire Emblem Fortune's Weave](../../dm/games/04007EF01F8AE000/README.md): 7 s after boot on the same route, exposure 3.035 and still climbing with 63 % of the frame near white, then 5.555 and settled with 0.1 %. The user's battle scenes, blown white before, look right after it.

### `[[primitive_id]]` in a specialized Metal 4 pipeline

- **Symptom:** a regular lattice of dots over the ground, and the light wrong: three of four layers of an interleaved ambient occlusion array stay empty, while the draws and their bindings look right.
- **Cause:** a Metal 4 pipeline specialized from a base with unspecialized colour state gives the fragment shader `[[primitive_id]]` 0 for every primitive. The same shader built whole counts properly. Measured in small test programs (16384 0 0 0 pixels per layer, against 4096 each), M6, macOS 27.2. The game writes the layer from the primitive ID, drawing four triangles. Ruled out: `gl_Layer`, view layers, clears, and the classic and Metal 4 API alone. This is a platform behaviour the host cannot change.
- **Fix:** the reflection records `reads_prim_id` (`raster.primitive_id`, `dmsh_build.py`), and `pso_build_ex` builds such a pipeline whole. Such pipelines give up the specialization's compile time, for these shaders only.
- **Prebuild:** `pre_desc` (the prebuild of a game's shader pairs) and `pso_offline.py` (the offline archive) leave pairs with such a fragment shader out, as no draw uses their base.
- **Check:** `t_image_primitive_id` draws four full-screen triangles that store into the layer of their primitive ID through the host's draw path (an attachment-less pass, a metallib loaded as game shaders are). Each of the four layers holds its own primitive's value; without the flag, layers 1 to 3 stay empty (macOS 27.0.1, M3 Pro). `test_pso_offline.py` checks that such a pair is left out.
- **Found on:** [Fire Emblem Fortune's Weave](../../dm/games/04007EF01F8AE000/README.md): `b6726be4afc44691`.

### Every start after play captures the whole pipeline archive again

- **Symptom:** the start after a play session spends minutes in the prepare step ("Preparing shaders") with MTLCompilerService at 700-980% CPU, and only a few bases are new: `pipeline prepare: 30838 lines (…) in 713.42 s on 10 workers before the first frame, progress screen shown; bases 262 compiled, 8346 from the archive, 637 reused`, then `pipeline archive compacted: 14 files of bases with a vertex layout gave 8543 bases to the files just written and were removed`.
- **Cause:** play writes a run file per flush (the timer's, 10 s after a burst of new bases; 13 to 273 in an Ocarina of Time session), and a compaction leaves a file per `ARCH_PERIOD_MAX` bases, 5 here. A few files of play bring the count over `PSO_ARCH_COMPACT`, and with every run file in the compaction its prewarm captures every base it takes from them again from the original shader functions ([the #342 workaround](shaders.md#a-compaction-flush-that-crashes-then-crashes-every-start)), a full compile each: 9 files of play beside a compaction's 5 made the next start capture 8,543 bases again. A start 6 minutes after a compaction captured the same bases again in as long (654 s against 669 s). From the code, not seen: a compaction of more than 16,000 bases writes more than `PSO_ARCH_COMPACT` files itself and compacts again at every start.
- **Fix:** a run file's name holds how many pipelines it holds (`<seq>-<count>.mtl4a`), and compaction takes files by size tier, `PSO_ARCH_COMPACT` times the one below (under 8 pipelines, 64, 512, then up to a full period): when a tier holds more than `PSO_ARCH_COMPACT` files, its files and those of every smaller tier are compacted (`arch_compact_pick`), and a base found in another file is used as it is (`arch_compact_takes`). Only the compacted files are removed. A full period's file is never compacted, since a compaction would write it again as it was. A file named before, or with a count that is not all digits, counts as the largest tier below a full period, so an earlier compaction's full files stay; a pile of more than 8 such files is compacted once, as before. Cost, from the rule alone (a test runs 200 sessions of 30 files of 6 pipelines): a start captures again about what the last session added, 506 pipelines on average, where the old rule took the whole archive; now and then the tiers above it fill and merge: 16,380 pipelines at sessions 91 and 191 in that run, about 19 minutes of compiler work on this Mac's 10 workers by the 0.7 s figure (not measured). How large such a start can be depends on what play brings: a start takes every file of the tiers it compacts, however many. At most 17 files below a full period stayed open after any start in that run (a merge can leave 9 in the tier above until the next start), each about 0.06 ms of a lookup that misses in it. A compaction never removes a full file, so one whose binaries a system update makes miss stays open until the archive folder is removed (from the code, not seen).
- **Check:** `t_pso_archive`: which files a start compacts, by tier, from their names (5 full files beside 9, 30 and 273 files of play, 9 full files, old and malformed names, each tier boundary), and 200 sessions by the rule alone; a compaction of two full periods' files and nine of one base captures only the nine again and keeps both full files, and the next start takes every base with none compiled; a compute pipeline in a file left out is not captured again. a merge that makes a ninth file in the tier above, taken at the next start. `test_pso_archive.py`, in fresh processes: the same compaction and the start after it; nine files named before the count compacted once, then not again. In the game (Ocarina of Time on an M4 with 16 GB, macOS 27.2, main bd979ced with eed7c429, copies of the device archive with a 30,838-line list, stopped at the title): a compaction's 5 files and 9 files of play (16, 19, 4, 7, 12, 5, 7, 6 and 11 pipelines, 5 under 8 and 4 under 64, so no tier overflows), with 262 bases new to the list: 713.42 s capturing 8,543 bases again before, 23.58 s compiling only the 262 with the change, and 2.35 s the start after. An earlier revision of the rule, the same for these files: after a real 17-minute session (29 files) the next start took 24.9 s capturing 255, the one after 3.0 s; an archive of 547 files named before compacted once, 669 s, then 3.5 s.
- **See also:** [Metal's archiver store filled by one flush period](#metals-archiver-store-filled-by-one-flush-period).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) on an M4 (16 GB, macOS 27.2 26B123).

### Metal's archiver store filled by one flush period

- **Symptom:** hundreds of `[RED] background render pipeline creation failed: error: cannot commit transaction no. '50463' on '…/com.apple.gpuarchiver/gpuarchiver-…/PersistentState': MDB_MAP_FULL: Environment mapsize limit reached` (or `cannot write 'UNITSMAPPINGS' database: MDB_MAP_FULL`), then `pipeline archive: compaction left the old files (the flush failed)`; the draws of those pipelines are missing and the frame rate falls (Ocarina of Time's Kakariko Village at 30-70 ms a frame, 1,502 such lines in one session).
- **Cause:** a pipeline the run captures for its archive is compiled through the flush period's Metal 4 compiler, bound to that period's pipeline data set serializer (`ep->comp`, `arch_capture`). Metal keeps each serializer's state in an LMDB environment of its own, a `gpuarchiver-*` folder under `com.apple.gpuarchiver` in the user cache folder, with a fixed map size of 10 MB (the `PersistentState` meta page). Once it is full every compile through that compiler fails, not only the capture, and the host took the error as the pipeline's: the pipeline was never built, and the period's file was not written either. A period had no size limit while a compacting prewarm ran: the timer's flushes waited for the prewarm's end so that the recaptured bases would make one file, and a prewarm of 40,000 rows put 8,693 bases into one period. Periods of 3,508 bases were written whole.
- **Fix:** a period is handed over at its 2,000th capture (`ARCH_PERIOD_MAX`, `arch_flush_from`), during a compacting prewarm too, so each gets a fresh environment; a compaction now writes a file per period and removes the old files once every period that may hold recaptured bases is written, and keeps them when one was not written or a base was not captured again. A pipeline the capture refuses is built by the plain compiler (`g_comp4`), since the archive is a cache, never a condition of drawing; the failing period is handed over once, and a failure in a period that has captured nothing (so not a full store) stops capturing for the run, said in red. A pipeline both compilers refuse returns the plain compiler's error, and costs a second compile.
- **Check:** `t_pso_archive`: concurrent bases with a failing capture compiler all get a pipeline and the period is handed over once; a failure in a new period stops the captures; both compilers refusing returns the error; a compaction with a period of 4 writes a file per period and keeps the old files until its last period is written. `t_pso_stall`: a base Apple's compiler cannot build fails on both compilers and leaves capturing on. In the log, `pipeline archive: capturing a render pipeline failed after N in its flush period`.
- **See also:** [Metal's build folders left by pipeline archive flushes](shaders.md#metals-build-folders-left-by-pipeline-archive-flushes): the folders stay on the Mac; they take disk space but do not fill later runs' stores, each period making a new one.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) on an M3 Pro.

### A cold start leaves a file per timer flush, and the next start compacts them all

- **Symptom:** the start after a cold start spends minutes in the prepare step ("Preparing shaders"), with MTLCompilerService at 700-980% CPU, yet the log says no base was compiled: `pipeline prepare: 30838 lines (…) in 761.61 s on 10 workers before the first frame, progress screen shown; bases 0 compiled, 8608 from the archive, 637 reused`, then `pipeline archive compacted: 80 files of bases with a vertex layout gave 8859 bases to the files just written and were removed` ([issue #1747](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1747): a cold start of 457 s, then a warm one of 309 s with 0 compiled).
- **Cause:** the timer's flush (10 s after a burst of new bases) ran during a prewarm that does not compact, so a cold start wrote a file every 10 s: 80 files on Ocarina of Time here, 43 in #1747. More than `PSO_ARCH_COMPACT` run files make the next start a compaction, and its prewarm captures every base it takes from them again from the original shader functions ([the #342 workaround](shaders.md#a-compaction-flush-that-crashes-then-crashes-every-start)), a full compile each. A capture counted neither as compiled nor apart from the lookups, so the prepare line read 0 compiled.
- **Fix:** the timer's flushes wait for the prewarm's end whether it compacts or not (`arch_flush_from`), as they already did during a compacting one, so a prewarm writes a file per `ARCH_PERIOD_MAX` bases. The prepare line says how many bases were captured again, `pipeline bases so far` also their time. What a prewarm holds unwritten is written when the player quits (`mtl_arch_save` from `atexit`, for ⌘Q, closing the window and `exit()`: it waits up to 10 s for the period's compiles and the writes, a file of 900 bases having taken 1.8-4 s; `dm_gfx_quit` in the graceful exit, within its 1.5 s) and starts writing when the app goes to the background or the window is minimized (`dm_gfx_suspend`). A write the exit cuts short leaves no `compacting` marker, which would turn compaction off on that system build (only this run's marker goes; a crashed run's stays). Only a process killed in its prewarm (a crash, `SIGKILL`) still leaves up to a period's captures unwritten (`ARCH_PERIOD_MAX`, plus compute pipelines still queued), built again at the next start. A cold start of more than `PSO_ARCH_COMPACT` x `ARCH_PERIOD_MAX` (16,000) bases still writes more than `PSO_ARCH_COMPACT` files, and the next start compacts them (from the code, not seen).
- **Check:** `t_pso_archive`: nine new bases in a prewarm with a timer flush after each make one file per full period of 4 and one for the rest, 3 files, no more than `PSO_ARCH_COMPACT`; a compaction counts its recaptured bases and their time. `test_pso_archive.py`, in fresh processes: that cold start, then a next start that compacts nothing and builds nothing; a cold start killed in its prewarm after 6 bases (`_exit`), which leaves the full period's file alone, so the next start takes those 4 and builds the other 5; and one that quits there (`exit()`), which writes the other 2 as well, so the next start takes all 6 and builds the other 3, leaving an earlier run's `compacting` marker in place. In the game (Ocarina of Time on an M4 with 16 GB, macOS 27.2, main bd979ced with eed7c429, copies of the device archive's prebuilt files with a 30,838-line list, stopped at the title): before, the cold start took 861.94 s writing 80 run files and the next start captured 8,859 pipelines again, 761.61 s before the first frame; with the change the cold start took 716.20 s writing 5 files (4 full periods and the rest) and the next start 3.30 s, with no compaction (one run each, nothing else running; an earlier pair with a build overlapping both cold starts: 854 and 725 s, then 736 and 3.67 s).
- **See also:** [Metal's archiver store filled by one flush period](#metals-archiver-store-filled-by-one-flush-period).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) on an M4 (16 GB, macOS 27.2 26B123); #1747 on an M4 Pro.

### Archive lookups asked every file, so a few files made a start compile every base again

- **Symptom:** a start after some play spends minutes in the prepare step and the log says `pipeline archive compacted: N files of bases with a vertex layout gave M bases to the files just written and were removed`: Ocarina of Time on an M4, 9 files of play beside a compaction's 5 made the next start capture 8,543 bases again, 713 s before the first frame (PR #1880); after a cold start's 80 files, 762 s ([A cold start leaves a file per timer flush](#a-cold-start-leaves-a-file-per-timer-flush-and-the-next-start-compacts-them-all), [issue #1747](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1747)).
- **Cause:** a lookup asked every file of the pipeline archive in turn, about 0.06 ms for each file the pipeline is not in, so every flush's file made each later lookup dearer, and more than `PSO_ARCH_COMPACT` (8) run files made a start a compaction. A compaction compiles every base it takes from the old files again from the original shader functions, because importing them through lookup archives crashes Metal ([the #342 workaround](shaders.md#a-compaction-flush-that-crashes-then-crashes-every-start)), a full compile each. So a rule that saved a few seconds of lookups (80 files: about 4.8 ms per miss, 2-4 s over a 9,000-base prewarm on 10 workers) cost minutes, and play, writing a file per timer flush, brought it on after a session or two. [Every start after play captures the whole pipeline archive again](#every-start-after-play-captures-the-whole-pipeline-archive-again) limits what a compaction takes; this entry is the lookups that made the file count matter.
- **Fix:** an index beside each archive file (`<file>.keys`, `arch_write_file`): the keys of the pipelines in it, and whether every pipeline in it has one. A key digests what the pipeline's Metal 4 descriptor sets (`arch_key`): its functions by library (the file it came from, under the shader folder: `lib_from_file` names it), entry name and constant values (by value: Metal has no getter for them, but their class describes and hashes them by value), the vertex layout, the colour attachments and every other field. A start reads every index into one table, newest file first, and opens the files in parallel (`pso4_init`); a lookup (`arch_lookup`: render bases, compute, task / mesh pipelines, the prebuild) asks the file the index names alone, and a miss asks none. A pipeline with no key (a library with no name: embedded kernels, diagnostic overrides) and a file with no index (written before it, or renamed) go through the files without a complete index, as before. Which files a compaction takes stays the size-tier rule (`arch_compact_pick`; `arch_compact_takes` now finds a file among every run file, `g_arch_run`, indexed or not), and a compacted file's index goes with it: the file count no longer costs lookups, only the start's opening of the files (in parallel) and the disk, which the tiers bound. A key the index names a file for is looked up there alone: a miss (an OS update that changed the GPU compiler, a library changed under the same name with a new chain) compiles it, and the new, newer file holds it from the next start on. A field the host starts setting on a pipeline descriptor must go into the key too, or two pipelines that differ only there share one, and one of them is compiled and written again at every start.
- **Check:** `t_pso_archive`: the first file's index holds its base's key, complete; the same pipeline gives the same key, another layout another, constant values by their values whatever the order they were set in, and a library with no name no key. `test_pso_archive.py`, in fresh processes: after a cold start's 3 indexed files the next start asks one file for each base it finds and none for the 5 it does not (also after a cut-short and a quit cold start); a compaction of 9 one-base files leaves one file with its index and removes the 9 with theirs; files renamed to the names of before lose their index and are asked in turn (the tier tests). In the game (ŌKAMI HD on an M4 Pro, macOS 27.2, its package split again for keyed records, a 152-line prewarm list, a fresh archive, two starts each): the first start compiled its 41 bases into one indexed file; a second start that asked for the same pipelines took all 41 from the archive (twice; and a third start, after a second had written the 4 it lacked to a file of its own, took 41 of 41). Some second starts compiled 2, 4 or 8 again: those pipelines paired the same pixel shaders with another vertex library (`a7537d4f1786cfda` where the first start used `c59f90…` and `b555f7…`), so no file held them and asking every file would have missed them too; which vertex library ŌKAMI HD's prewarm takes varies between starts (open, not from the index).
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time](../../dm/games/0400486024DEC000/README.md) (PRs #1879 and #1880, issue #1747).

### A specialization descriptor iOS 26.0 returns autoreleased

- **Symptom:** with Metal 4 pipelines on (`DM_PSO_MTL4=1`), the iOS app crashes before its first frame, right after `pipeline prewarm list … lines`: `CRASH sig=11 pc=objc_release+0x10`, `bt: objc_autoreleasePoolPop warm_task __warm_prepare_block_invoke`, on several prepare workers at once. The Mac runs the same pipelines.
- **Cause:** `newRenderPipelineDescriptorForSpecialization` promises a +1 object by its name, so ARC releases it once at the end of `pso_build_ex`. On iOS 26.0 (23A341) the driver returns it autoreleased. On an iPad Pro M4 the method is `-[AGXG16GFamilyRenderPipeline newRenderPipelineDescriptorForSpecialization]` in `AGXMetalG16G_B0`. So ARC's release freed it, and the warm task's pool released it again. Under LLDB on the iPad:
  - the descriptor sits in the autorelease pool when the call returns (`_objc_autoreleasePoolPrint`);
  - `NSZombieEnabled=YES` reports `-[MTL4RenderPipelineDescriptor release]: message sent to deallocated instance`.
  That one release freed it, so the driver keeps no reference of its own. macOS 27.0 (26A428, M4 Pro) returns it retained. There, the direct call made the same prewarm's 34 specializations without a crash.
- **Fix:** `spec_desc` (`nvn_mtl.m`) finds out on its first call how the driver returns the descriptor. A weak reference survives the call's own autorelease pool only if the caller owns the descriptor. After that it takes the descriptor the way the driver gives it, so the caller owns exactly one reference either way. The answer holds for the process: every base comes from the one device's compiler or its archives. The `pipeline bases so far` line names it: `their descriptors returned retained` or `returned autoreleased`. `t_pso_stall` specializes through it too.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=01005CA01580E000 check_t_pso_archive` runs `spec_desc` against two stand-in bases, one that returns the descriptor retained and one that returns it autoreleased. On the first call and on later ones, the descriptor outlives every pool and is freed once dropped. The previous direct call dies on the autoreleased stand-in. Axiom Verge with `DM_PSO_MTL4=1`:
  - on the iPad, it crashed on every start before the fix. Now it runs at 60 fps: the cold start compiled 18 bases and made 25 specializations, and later starts take the bases from the archive. The log says `returned autoreleased`;
  - on the Mac, it says `returned retained`.
- **Found on:** Axiom Verge 1.0.3 (`0100052004384000`, its profile a separate change) with the launcher's **Metal 4 pipelines** on: an iPhone 16 Pro Max (iOS 26.0, LiveContainer) and an iPad Pro 11-inch (M4, iPadOS 26.0, from Xcode and in LiveContainer) crashed the same way.

### NVN2's DLSS on MetalFX

- **Symptom:** a Switch 2 game calls `nvnCommandBufferEvaluateDlss` and its builder functions, which the host did not have.
- **Cause:** NVN2 has DLSS as a part of the API, a neural network that runs on NVIDIA's GPU, with no counterpart on the Mac.
- **Fix:** `hle_nvn.c` takes the builder, the network and the parameters (the network's texture IDs and storage are the driver's own needs and are dropped), and answers `GetOptimalSettings` from the method (0: 2x, 1: 3x, 2: 1x). The replay's `OP_DLSS` runs MetalFX's temporal scaler (`dlss_eval`, `nvn_mtl.m`) over colour, depth and motion vectors, with the jitter and a history reset, falling back to a bilinear draw when there is no scaler. This replaces the upscaler, so the picture is close to the game's, not the same.
- **Encoding thread:** with `DM_NVN_ENC_THREAD` (the iOS default) the replay's command buffer is the recording's stand-in, which has none of MetalFX's calls: the host stopped at the first evaluation (`deferred encoding: DmRecCB has no stand-in for -useResidencySet:`). The scaler's state and its encode are a block run on the encoding thread with the real command buffer (`rec_cb_block` in `nvn_rec.h`), as for [a game's own upscaler](#a-games-own-upscaler-drawn-by-metalfx). Check: `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=040074A01BF12000 check_t_dlss_mfx` (two evaluations through one scaler in a submission, the thread off and on). Not run on a game with the thread.
- **Open:** the jitter's sign has not been checked and the bias mask is only logged; no ghosting has been seen.
- **Found on:** [Fire Emblem Fortune's Weave](../../dm/games/04007EF01F8AE000/README.md): `gfxa::TaskDLSS`, 1024x576 to 2048x1152.

### The DLSS network resized

- **Symptom:** `[RED] NVN unimplemented function called: nvnDlssNetworkResize (x0=… x1=0x300 x2=0x1b0)` at the title.
- **Cause:** NVN2 lets a game resize a DLSS network instead of making a new one. The replay takes each evaluation's sizes from its parameters and textures, so the network keeps nothing of it.
- **Fix:** `nvnDlssNetworkResize` succeeds, logging the first size (`hle_nvn.c`).
- **Found on:** [Star Fox](../../dm/games/0400E88025B1A000/README.md): 768x432, once; its evaluations run 768x432 to 768x432.

### A game's own upscaler drawn by MetalFX

- **Symptom:** a Switch 2 game's frame in its heavy scenes is dominated by one compute dispatch between the render-resolution passes and the output-resolution ones: Donkey Kong Bananza's upscaler takes 3.5-5 ms of a 24 ms GPU frame on an M1 in handheld mode (960x540 → 1920x1080, `DM_NVN_GPUPROF`), and the game cannot reach its 60 fps interval there.
- **Cause:** the upscaler is the game's own network (agl's NDSR, `agl_ndsr.pck`), written for the Switch 2's tensor cores: its HMMA matrix products are emulated ([Ampere HMMA through simdgroup_matrix](shaders.md#ampere-hmma-through-simdgroup_matrix)), and its shared-memory matrix loads move their row addresses between lanes with shuffles (41 % of its time on the M1). MetalFX's spatial scaler does the same job, the same sizes, in 0.97 ms.
- **Fix:** an opt-in swap, the player's choice since the look changes. The game's upscaler shaders are the compute shaders its shader map found in `agl_ndsr.pck` (`upscaler_keys` in `dm/games/game.py`, the header's `DM_GAME_UPSCALER_KEYS`; Bananza: 24 variants, each one texture in at slot 0 and one image out at slot 1; Splatoon Raiders: 20); the launcher shows **Game's upscaler** (`DM_UPSCALER`: `game` by default, `metalfx`) only for games that have some. With `metalfx`, `up_mfx_encode` reads the two rectangles from push-constant bank 24 (`S.push[1]`). Each rectangle contains an origin and an inclusive last texel relative to that origin. For Bananza's R11G11B10F scene colour, `copy_t2t_draw` draws the input rectangle into an RGBA16F texture at the origin. The Metal 4 spatial scaler processes it in the colour mode the shader's code shows ([An upscaler's colour read from its code](#an-upscalers-colour-read-from-its-code)), then a second draw places the result in the output rectangle. Compatible RGBA16F bindings can go directly through MetalFX when both rectangles cover the whole textures and the output uses private storage. On the encoding thread the recording holds a block that encodes the scaler into the real command buffer (`rec_cb_block` in `nvn_rec.h`; [NVN2's DLSS on MetalFX](#nvn2s-dlss-on-metalfx) goes the same way). Four size pairs are cached for dynamic resolution. Invalid rectangles, shrinking or equal-sized pairs, indirect dispatches and unsupported bindings or scaler sizes keep the game's shader.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=040074A01BF12000 check_t_upscaler_mfx`: a named shader's 960x540 → 1920x1080 dispatch fills the image through MetalFX. A second dispatch converts a cropped R11G11B10F input with a highlight above 1.0 and checks every pixel inside and outside an offset output rectangle. It runs with the encoding thread off and on. Equal sizes and an unnamed shader keep the shader, and without `DM_UPSCALER=metalfx` nothing changes (Metal validation stops MetalFX's Metal 4 scaler on a barrier setting of its own with no public API, so that run is without it). `test_game_profile.py` checks the keys taken from a shader map and `test_settings.py` that the launcher offers the choice to Bananza and not to a game without the network. **Open:** a standalone Metal 4 MetalFX HDR test on M1 intermittently returned non-finite pixels at small input sizes (64x36 and 320x180). The packed-format regression also saw an intermittent colour error at handheld resolution. Check picture quality and frame time against the game's network in a heavy area. The network's own cost is the translator's LDSM / MOVM emulation, open in [Ampere HMMA through simdgroup_matrix](shaders.md#ampere-hmma-through-simdgroup_matrix).
- **Found on:** [Donkey Kong Bananza](../../dm/games/040074A01BF12000/README.md): NDSR `7629d857a0a93833` in the heavy areas, 1676x943 → 2304x1296 docked (after three passes of its own at the render resolution, which stay), 960x540 → 1920x1080 handheld.

### An upscaler's colour read from its code

- **Symptom:** with `DM_UPSCALER=metalfx`, a game's picture is washed out wherever it upscales: lifted shadows and low contrast, the scene only, with the UI drawn after it correct; with `DM_UPSCALER=game`, or at a size the game does not upscale, it is right.
- **Cause:** the swap ran every variant of agl's NDSR network in MetalFX's HDR mode, taking input and output as linear. That is true only of some. Each variant shows its colour in its sRGB curves: Bananza's pack (header word `0x300`) encodes its linear input right after reading it (0.0031308, 1 / 2.4, as floats); Splatoon Raiders' pack (`0x40100`) has no such step, because the colour it reads is encoded already. Within each pack the output differs by variant: no decode at the end, so the output stays encoded (half of Splatoon Raiders', a third of Bananza's), or a decode to linear (0.04045, 2.4; in Splatoon Raiders' half-float code 0.0404358, 2.3984375 and 0.0774536 for 1 / 12.92). So MetalFX passed Splatoon Raiders' encoded colour through, into an image the following passes read as linear.
- **Fix:** the host reads the colour from each named shader's code when the program loads (`up_mfx_read_color` in `prog_get`, `up_mfx_color`): the curves' constants are immediates, whole bytes in the code, and count from 8 uses, far fewer than a curve per channel of every output tile. No profile key, no chain change, no shader rebuild. An encode and a decode in floats are linear input and output: HDR mode, as before. A half-float decode with no encode is sRGB input and linear output: perceptual mode into RGBA8Unorm (the network clamps to 0-1 before its decode, so the range is the same), and the draw into the game's image reads it through an RGBA8Unorm_sRGB view, which decodes it; always through the scaler's textures. Any other shape keeps the game's shader, with a log line: a variant whose output stays encoded, and shapes no pack has shown. Each colour has its own scaler in the size cache.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=0400380020056000 check_t_upscaler_mfx`: the classifier on synthetic code in each shape, and an sRGB input, full and as a packed subrectangle, comes out decoded, with linear input at the same sizes not decoded. The classifier against the translated shaders of all 44 variants of the two packs: no mismatch (Bananza 16 linear, 8 kept; Splatoon Raiders 10 sRGB, 10 kept). **Open:** Mario Kart World's 30 variants have not been read; whatever shape they are, an unknown one keeps its shader.
- **Found on:** [Splatoon Raiders](../../dm/games/0400380020056000/README.md) ([#1156](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/issues/1156)), the first level with `c1b8edc09e3313b1` and `30d8ee33de9dee85`: the washed-out picture reproduced, and with the fix the sky and scene means match the game's upscaler within 0.6 of 255 per channel, against about 50 too high before.

### Metal 4 does not order operations inside one encoder

- **Symptom:** tall blue / textured panels that flickered frame to frame: the fountain's vertex shader pushed the water surface to the top of the screen.
- **Cause:** the host batches consecutive copies, fills and inline uniform updates (`UpdateUniformBuffer`) into one compute encoder, and Metal 4 does not order operations inside one encoder. Mario writes the same uniform slot twice in a row each frame (4 bytes of −1, then 16 bytes of wave coefficients); without an intra-encoder barrier the writes occasionally landed in reverse order, the fountain's GPU wave simulation (a 512×1 RGBA16F texture updated by render → copy back → splash pass every frame) ran with −1 as its self coefficient and diverged to ±65504.
- **Fix:** `blit_buf` now inserts `barrierAfterEncoderStages` when an operation reads or writes a guest range an earlier operation in the same encoder wrote; texture copies always get one. Render passes have their own encoder with full barriers, so they were not affected; compute dispatches share the copy encoder and always get a barrier, as texture copies do (Hyrule Warriors: Age of Calamity, [Compute dispatches share the copy encoder](#compute-dispatches-share-the-copy-encoder)).
- **Check:** `DM_NVN_BLIT_ORDER=0` turns the barriers off to reproduce.
- **Found on:** [Super Mario Bros. Wonder](../../dm/games/010015100B514000/README.md).

### Buffer copies through aliased Metal addresses

- **Symptom:** a buffer copy, inline update or fill completes, but a following copy through another Metal buffer over the same allocation reads stale bytes.
- **Cause:** Metal buffers over one allocation have distinct GPU addresses. Device visibility alone does not flush their alias caches. Sparse virtual pools also give the same physical bytes distinct guest addresses, so the copy encoder's guest-range dependency checks can miss a conflict entirely; barriers at encoder boundaries cannot order copies within that encoder.
- **Fix:** `blit_buf` marks encoders containing buffer copies, updates or fills; their start, intra-encoder and end barriers include `MTL4VisibilityOptionResourceAlias`. Operations touching a sparse virtual range (`nvn_sparse_alias`) receive conservative barriers against prior and subsequent operations, since a mapped run can span unrelated physical allocations. Ordinary buffers retain range-based batching. Texture-only and helper encoders keep their existing visibility options.
- **Check:** `make -C dm/gfx/tests GAME=010015100B514000 check_t_copy_alias` exercises two no-copy buffers over one allocation, physical-to-virtual, virtual-to-physical and virtual-to-virtual sparse aliases, copies, inline updates and both fill paths, within one encoder and across encoder boundaries, with direct and deferred encoding and Metal validation. On an M4 Pro, the original host failed 37 of 101 checks; [PR #200](https://github.com/seedds/nxnative/pull/200) alone left 24 failures from missed intra-encoder sparse dependencies; the complete fix passes all 101 in both encoding modes.
- **Found on:** synthetic regression using the sparse mapping mechanism of [Monster Hunter Rise](../../dm/games/0100B04011742000/README.md); the game and iPhone have not been rerun for this change. The no-copy buffer failure was reported by MacNativeGames (`803128e`) in PR #200 and reproduced here.

### Inline uniform updates inside a render pass

- **Symptom:** many render passes with one draw each and high submit time: Metroid Dread's cutscene frames had about 180 passes with one draw each (about 5 ms of submit time per frame on the Mac).
- **Cause:** the game updates uniforms inline (`CommandBufferUpdateUniformBuffer`) between draws, and every copy or update ended the host's render pass. This was correct but cost submit time.
- **Fix:** an inline uniform update inside a pass no longer ends it: it is deferred to the pass end (`DM_NVN_UB_DEFER=0` restores the old behaviour), and the draws after it read a shadow ([Partial-cover uniform shadows keep passes open](#partial-cover-uniform-shadows-keep-passes-open)); see [development.md, Power](../development.md#power).
- **Found on:** [Metroid Dread](../../dm/games/010093801237C000/README.md).
- **Note:** 04's Metroid Dread section (at `6a06877`) still described the old behaviour ("every copy or update ends the host's render pass"); the deferral came later (development.md's Power section), and it is now the default (`ubd_on` in `nvn_mtl.m`, off with `DM_NVN_UB_DEFER=0` or `DM_NVN_TBDR=1`).

### Partial-cover uniform shadows keep passes open

- **Symptom:** hundreds of render passes a frame and heavy attachment traffic when a game updates a small part of a large uniform block before each draw group.
- **Cause:** a draw whose uniform binding overlaps an update deferred in the open pass reads a shadow; when the deferred updates cover only part of what the shader can read, the rest must come from somewhere, and the pass used to end there (`nvn_mtl.m`, the comment above `ubd_on`).
- **Fix:** the shadows take the rest from memory (`ubp_get`, `DM_NVN_UB_PARTIAL`) and the per-shader read extents of `cb_extent.tsv` (written by a full `dmsh_build.py` run) keep those passes open. `DM_NVN_UBP_CHECK=1` compares every shadow with the GPU's memory.
- **Found on:** [The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the Wild](../../dm/games/01007EF00011E000/README.md) (2026-10-21, the user's Great Plateau save, Mac M4 Pro): a frame had about 386 render passes and loaded and stored about 900 MB of attachments (on an iPhone at 60 fps about 55 GB/s, near the memory's bandwidth); the GPU waits for each pass to finish before the next. 220 of the passes ended only because the game updates the first 64 bytes of a 64 KB vertex uniform block (`0x70c38c0000`) before each draw group while its shaders read up to 544 bytes of it (the terrain pixel shader `eb0afe555b86da79`, which indexes the block with an integer made from an interpolated value, reads the whole block). With the fix: 386 → about 165 passes, GPU p50 about 9.3 → 6.5 ms, submit 5.7 → 4.9 ms; `DM_NVN_UBP_CHECK=1` found every shadow equal to the GPU's memory (968,000 checked).
- **Note:** a storage buffer bound with size 0 once made every partly covered shadow after it in the submission refuse ([A storage buffer bound with size 0 counted as a write anywhere](#a-storage-buffer-bound-with-size-0-counted-as-a-write-anywhere)). What still splits BotW's big passes (a `DM_NVN_PASSDUMP` frame dump): the NintendoWare VFX GPU particles run one compute dispatch per emitter between draws (ten 800x450 half-resolution passes and two G-buffer passes, about 190 MB a frame; the G-buffer ones read the depth being drawn, so they cannot move ahead of the pass), and decals that sample the G-buffer right after the game sets it again. The decals sample targets of their own pass (Metal leaves that undefined; the Mac reads the stored content); `DM_NVN_FB_SNAP=all` would snapshot them at a large cost. Tried and dropped: `DM_NVN_TBDR=1` (10–15% fewer stores, more CPU, red ledger lines, and it turns the deferred uniform updates off), `DM_NVN_BARRIER=game` (no gain), same-target continuation outside TBDR mode (the decals end the pass anyway).

### A storage buffer bound with size 0 counted as a write anywhere

- **Symptom:** a scene runs at 20–27 fps where similar ones hold 60, with the GPU time about three times its usual (draw counts unchanged).
